The moment he stepped into the archive proper, a sound rose around him that had no single source and no single register — not screaming, exactly, though scream was the closest word his mind could reach for it, a chorus of voices layered so densely over each other that no individual one could be isolated from the whole. They came from the shelves themselves, from the ancient volumes stacked floor to ceiling in every direction, each one carrying some fragment of forbidden understanding too heavy for the vessel that had first recorded it, and the accumulated weight of all that understanding pressed outward now in a psychic din that made ordinary thought difficult to hold together.

He walked the first level slowly, one hand trailing along the shelves without quite touching the books themselves, reading titles in scripts his centuries of study should have prepared him for and hadn't. Some volumes bore no title at all, only a raw, unmarked binding that seemed to shrink faintly from his attention when he lingered too long near it. Others pulsed with a faint, sourceless heat, as though the knowledge bound inside had never fully cooled from whatever violence had first produced it. He passed shelf after shelf without daring to pull a single volume free, understanding, in the marrow-deep way a hunter understands a trap before he sees its teeth, that this level held nothing worth the cost of lingering to read it.

✦

He pressed deeper anyway, one careful step at a time, and the vision found him somewhere in the archive's third level, arriving without warning between one breath and the next.

He saw — not with his eyes, not in any sense his centuries of ordinary perception had prepared him to process — a universe that came before the one he knew. No order in it. No firmament, no separation of light from dark, no Word yet spoken to give any of it shape or law. Only a vast, formless churning, matter and meaning and time itself still undifferentiated from each other, chaos not as absence but as a kind of terrible, undirected fullness, everything that would ever exist compressed into a single ungoverned moment with no boundary yet drawn around any of it. He felt, more than saw, the specific horror of a reality with no judgment in it at all — no right, no wrong, no verdict ever rendered on anything, because nothing yet existed capable of rendering one.

He staggered, one hand finding a shelf to steady himself, and felt something in his own composure begin to give way beneath the vision's weight. It was not the scale of it that threatened to break him. He had stood in the presence of scale before, had watched entire realms tremble under the weight of decisions made far above his own station. It was the absence at the center of it — a reality with no verdict rendered anywhere inside it, a state of being his entire existence had been built, purpose and instinct alike, to stand in direct opposition to. He was a hunter of things that had escaped judgment. He had never once imagined a version of creation in which judgment itself had not yet been invented.

✦

He forced himself to move inside the vision, one deliberate step at a time, though nothing around him corresponded to the archive floor his boots still stood on somewhere beneath all of it. He thought of Gabriel's voice in the cold observatory, the plain, unadorned trust in it — I need that instinct in the Tower, not a library index — and repeated the words to himself, once, twice, a third time, until saying them took up more of his attention than the vision could. He was not here to understand the shape of a universe before judgment existed. He was here because a captain of Hell had died without a category to place his own death in, and because Gabriel needed to know what Belial had learned in this same terrible place before anyone else paid the price of that ignorance. He made himself take one more step, and then another, and let the mission decide where his feet actually landed instead of the vision.

The vision released him the way a hand finally opens after gripping too hard for too long, all at once and unevenly, and Sariel found himself on his knees on the archive's cold floor, breathing hard, blood running warm from one nostril in a thin, steady line he didn't bother to wipe away. His hands wouldn't stop shaking for the better part of a minute, a tremor that had nothing to do with cold and everything to do with whatever the vision had asked of muscles never built to carry weight like that. He had crossed psychic assaults before, in centuries of hunting the reincarnated across every corner of two worlds. He had never once crossed anything that left this particular residue behind — not pain, exactly, but the lingering sense of having glimpsed something his mind had no proper shelf to store afterward.

✦

He allowed himself, kneeling there in the Tower's screaming quiet, exactly one honest moment of doubt — not about the mission itself, which remained as clear and necessary as it had been in the observatory, but about whether the man who eventually walked back out of this archive would still recognize himself as fully the same one who had walked in. He had felt something shift in him during the vision, some fine, permanent adjustment to how he understood the shape of everything he'd ever fought to protect, and he did not yet know what that adjustment would cost him once the adrenaline of simply surviving it had faded.

He decided, in the end, that the question didn't matter enough to answer before he moved on. Whatever he became by the time this mission finished, he would become it in service of the same purpose that had sent him here in the first place, and a man could survive being changed by his work far more easily than he could survive failing it. He thought, briefly, of Gabriel's question in the observatory — whether he'd ever once found a reincarnated soul who remembered anything at all before he found them — and understood, kneeling here with blood still drying on his lip, that he had just been given his own small answer to it. Something vast had touched him and already begun slipping out of reach, and he suspected he would spend a long while afterward only half-remembering what it had actually shown him.

He wiped the blood from his lip with the back of one hand, pushed himself back to his feet, and turned deeper into the archive's endless, whispering shelves, following the same patient instinct that had carried him through every hunt he'd ever completed, toward whatever record of Belial's passage still waited somewhere ahead in the dark.

Chapter 21

A World Awakened

Belial felt the echo before any report reached him, a tremor rolling through the fabric of things that had nothing to do with Lament's familiar, permanent ache. He set the spear aside and sat with the sensation a long moment, turning it over the way he turned every unfamiliar thing that crossed his attention, cataloguing its shape against everything he already knew. It did not match the profile of any angel he'd ever fought. Angels announced themselves in ways he'd long since learned to read at a glance — the particular resonance of Heaven's authority stamped into every one of their strikes, unmistakable even at a distance. This carried something older beneath it, a note that predated the war itself, the same unplaceable quality that had first drawn his hunters toward the Grey City in the first place.

He had built his entire long ascent on the discipline of knowing precisely what he was facing before he committed anything of value to facing it. Tonight, for the first time in longer than he cared to admit even to himself, that discipline had produced nothing more than an educated guess, and he despised having to act on one.

"Send everyone," he said, when the report finally arrived, his voice flat with a certainty that allowed no argument. "Whatever burned in that facility tonight, I want confirmation before Heaven finishes drawing its own conclusions. We are no longer chasing a rumor."

He turned Lament once against his shoulder as the sorcerer withdrew to carry out the order, feeling its familiar hum sharpen briefly beneath his palm, and thought of the two signals his hunters had been tracking so carefully these past weeks — the one that slept, patient and unhurried near Navarion, and the one that had just announced itself to the entire world with a violence Belial had not authorized and could not yet explain. He had told his commander to watch the one that slept and chase the one that screamed. The one that screamed had just proven considerably louder than even he had expected.

✦

The same tremor reached Heaven within the same breath, and Raphael was the first to name it properly, standing before the war room's great star-map with an expression Gabriel hadn't seen on his face in longer than he cared to measure. "That is not an angel's justice," Raphael said, quiet enough that only Gabriel, standing close beside him, fully caught the words. "I would know an angel's justice if I felt it from the other side of creation. This is older. Primordial, if I had to give it a name I trusted." He turned to Gabriel, something that might almost have been hope breaking through the careful composure he usually wore like armor. "Whatever Sariel finds in that Tower, I suspect it's about to matter a great deal sooner than either of us expected."

Gabriel said nothing for a long moment, his eyes fixed on the star-map's newest point of light, flaring bright over a city the records simply labeled Grey. He thought of Camael's report, of a mortal who had unmade a captain of Hell without raising a single weapon, and felt something settle into place in him that had nothing to do with certainty and everything to do with the particular, cautious relief of a theory finally acquiring evidence to stand on. He had sent Sariel into Hell's own oldest archive on the strength of a single unnamed mortal and a captain's borrowed testimony. Tonight's flare did not prove Sariel's mission was necessary. It proved, at the very least, that Gabriel had not sent him chasing a shadow.

"Should we move on it," Raphael asked, "before Hell does?"

"We don't yet know what 'it' is," Gabriel said. "I am not willing to send soldiers after a signature none of us can properly name. Not until Sariel gives us something more solid to stand on than a light on a map."

✦

On the mountain no mortal foot had ever climbed, the figure who had not moved in longer than anyone living could count felt the sharper of his two threads flare bright against the weave and, for the first time in longer than his own stillness could easily measure, chose to act rather than simply watch.

He did not reach across the whole shivering distance the way he had once reached for a lesser thread, weeks ago, to turn aside a clumsy scout before it ever closed the gap. This was different — not a danger to redirect, but an exposure to soften. He drew something dimmer around the burning thread instead, muting the signal without extinguishing it, so that anything watching from either Heaven or Hell would find only a fraction of the light actually there. He recognized something in the pattern of that light he could not have explained to anyone who might have asked, a resonance that answered some old, unfinished question in himself before he'd consciously chosen to let it. He did not examine that recognition too closely.

The cost arrived a half-breath later, the way it always did — a fine new crack tracing itself through the stone beneath him, wider this time than the last one had been, the sustained work of a veil demanding more of him than a single turned-aside scout ever had. He paid it without hesitation, and let the veil settle, and returned to his stillness with one thing quietly changed in the shape of his long watching.

✦

Arin was three streets clear of the Grey City's ruined tower before the shaking in his hands finally settled, and he did not stop moving even then, some old professional instinct insisting that a man who had just announced his existence to two entire warring realms had earned exactly zero minutes of rest to process what that announcement might cost him. He kept to alleys, to service corridors, to every unwatched seam a city this joyless still managed to offer a man who knew how to look for them, and felt, beneath the exhaustion, a new and unfamiliar wariness settling into every choice he made.

He was being hunted now by something considerably more organized than the ordinary demons he'd spent a month learning to track. He understood that plainly, without needing anyone to confirm it for him — the pulse that had rolled out of him in that corridor was not the kind of thing either side of an ancient war simply let go unexamined. He kept walking anyway, putting distance between himself and the tower's wreckage with the same patient, unhurried discipline he'd built an entire career on, and told himself, without much conviction, that whatever came looking for him next would have to work considerably harder to actually find him than it had taken him to be found tonight.

He allowed himself, once he'd finally put enough distance behind him to slow his pace, to think properly about what he'd become in that corridor — the voice that had used his mouth, the light that had unmade a captain without leaving so much as ash behind. He had no framework for it, no old professional category that fit what he'd felt happen inside his own chest. He only knew that whatever had spoken through him had called itself by a name, and that some part of him, hearing it, had recognized the sound the way a man recognizes his own handwriting on a letter he doesn't remember writing.

He found a doorway deep enough to hide in before the exhaustion finally caught up with him, and sat there a long while turning the word over in his mind, testing its weight the way he might have tested an unfamiliar blade. It did not feel like a stranger's name. It did not feel entirely like his own, either. He filed the contradiction away with everything else he'd learned to carry without understanding across the last month, and let himself, finally, close his eyes for what little rest the night still owed him.

✦

Iofiel brought her findings to Gabriel two days later, the energy signature from the Grey City laid out across a table between them in careful, precise notation that told a story she clearly hadn't enjoyed writing. "The cycle did to this soul exactly what it does to every soul," she said, without preamble, her staff held close the way Gabriel had seen it before, on the evenings something troubled her more than she wanted to admit aloud. "Full passage. Full forgetting. Whoever this was before, the cycle wrapped them in the same clean amnesia it wraps around everyone, and started them fresh, the way it's supposed to."

"And yet," Gabriel said, reading the rest of it in her expression before she finished saying it herself.

"And yet something in this particular soul resisted forgetting completely." She set the notation down, and for once let the worry show plainly rather than dressing it in professional distance. "The laws I've spent my whole existence protecting were never written with a soul that remembers how to scream in mind, even if it doesn't remember what it was screaming about. I don't have a category for that. I'm not certain the cycle itself has one either."

"Does that mean the cycle is failing?"

Iofiel considered the question longer than its size seemed to warrant, and when she finally answered, there was no comfortable certainty in it at all. "Either it's failing," she said, "or it's doing exactly what it was always meant to do, and none of us ever fully understood what that was supposed to look like until tonight." She hesitated, then added the part she'd clearly been holding back since she'd first sat down. "I keep thinking of the sword we still haven't found, and the man who used to carry it, still walking around somewhere with none of this in his memory. I don't have proof they're the same kind of unraveling. I would be lying if I told you I thought they weren't."

Gabriel didn't answer that directly. He only nodded once, filing it away the same careful way he'd learned to file every suspicion she brought him that wasn't yet ready to be called a certainty.

✦

Iofiel had spent the two days since isolating the Grey City anomaly teaching the star-map's own watchers what to actually search for — not the clean, ordinary signature of an unremarkable soul, but the particular resistant flaw she'd catalogued in the Grey City signature, the scream a soul wasn't supposed to remember how to make. Retuned to that single stubborn variable, the star-map in the celestial war room held two points of light that night, close enough to the same hemisphere that a careless glance might have mistaken them for a single flare instead of two separate ones — one near a quiet university called Navarion, dim but unmistakably present now that someone had finally thought to look properly. The other burned brighter still over the Grey City, restless in a way the first point wasn't, though no visible thread yet connected the two of them across the star-map's careful grid.

No one in the room said anything about it. The aides who usually filled the war room's quiet hours with routine chatter fell silent the moment the second point flared into place beside the first, their conversations trailing off unfinished, their attention drifting toward Gabriel and staying there.

Gabriel stood before the map a while longer than his usual review required, his own reflection faint against the glass, and let his eyes move once more between the two burning points before he finally turned back to the stack of reports still waiting on his desk, carrying with him the particular, unspoken weight of a man who had just watched a question quietly become larger than the answer he'd been hoping to find for it. Two points, still unconnected on the map's careful grid. He said nothing about either of them. He only looked a moment longer than he needed to, and let the silence stand.

Chapter 22

The Riddle of the Laughing Smith

The light kept sinking regardless of what Mikel decided to do about it, and he made his peace with that the way he'd made his peace with every other impossible constraint this island had handed him since the fog first swallowed the mainland behind him. He returned to the anvil with the last honest hour of daylight he could count on, and laid both hands flat against its worn, ancient face this time instead of one, some instinct insisting that the earlier, tentative touch hadn't been enough to ask the stone whatever question it was still refusing to answer.

The warmth came first, faint and impossible in metal this old, this thoroughly abandoned — a ghost of heat with no visible source, the residue of a forge fire that should have gone cold centuries before anyone alive today had been born. He held his palms against it a long moment, feeling it pulse very faintly beneath his skin, patient in a way that felt less like coincidence and more like something old finally noticing it had company.

He thought of every ruin he'd ever studied properly, every excavation where stone this age had long since surrendered whatever warmth its makers had once poured into it. None of them had prepared him for this. This forge did not feel abandoned the way ruins felt abandoned. It felt, beneath his palms, more like something merely resting between one use and the next. He glanced once toward the tree line, gauging how much of the fading light he still had left to spend here, and pressed his hands more firmly against the stone.

He was aware, distantly, of how this would read in any paper he might one day write about it — a historian abandoning every principle of careful, skeptical inquiry to press both palms flat against a rock and wait for a vision to arrive. He had stopped caring, somewhere in the last several months, whether the discipline that had built his entire career still applied to whatever this search actually was. The old rules had been built for evidence that behaved the way evidence was supposed to behave. Nothing about this island had behaved that way since the moment the fog first swallowed the boat.

✦

The vision arrived without warning, breaking over him the way a wave breaks over a swimmer who has misjudged the tide. He had expected, if he'd expected anything at all, something closer to the somber weight of his own recurring dreams — grief, judgment, the crushing scale of divine consequence he'd spent months learning to associate with anything touching this search. What found him instead was joy.

Raw, chaotic, undirected joy, energy that moved without purpose or apology, sparks that laughed as they scattered rather than burning with the grim purposefulness he'd expected from any weapon born of war. He felt, more than saw, a presence at the center of it all — not a figure exactly, nothing his mind could resolve into features or form, only a raw, delighted laughter that rang through the vision like a bell struck in genuine, unguarded pleasure. It bore no resemblance to the reverent, terrible creation myths he'd spent a career studying. This felt like something made for the sheer, uncomplicated pleasure of making it.

He saw fragments rather than a coherent scene — metal folding under a hammer that struck not out of anger but out of something closer to delight, sparks arcing away in patterns too playful to belong to any serious act of forging he'd ever read described. There was no solemnity in any of it, no sense of a sacred duty being carefully discharged. Whoever had stood at this anvil once had worked the way a child works at something purely for the pleasure of watching it take shape, careless of consequence, untroubled by weight. It was, Mikel understood even as the vision continued to unspool around him, the single strangest creation myth he had ever encountered in a lifetime spent cataloguing them.

✦

The laughter did not feel entirely foreign to him, and that unsettled Mikel more than the vision's strangeness ever could have. He thought he knew this sound. Not consciously, not in any way he could have explained to another scholar without sounding like a man losing his grip on his own credibility, but somewhere beneath conscious memory, in the same buried country his recurring dreams had always come from, something in him strained toward this laughter as though it might belong to the same figure who had forged the spear that haunted his nightmares — the dark, judging blade that answered grief with cruelty rather than relief. He could not have said, if pressed, whether that was recognition or simply a mind too tired and too far from home inventing a connection it wanted to find.

He sat with that half-formed suspicion a long moment after the vision released him, turning it over with the careful discipline of a man who had built his whole career on not mistaking coincidence for pattern. He tried, methodically, the way he'd once have approached a disputed source, to trace the sound back to some ordinary origin — a half-remembered lecture, a book he'd skimmed years ago and forgotten reading. Nothing fit cleanly. He could not prove the laughter belonged to the same buried country his dreams came from. He also could not shake the feeling that it did.

For the first time since this search had begun, the question that mattered wasn't what is this. It was why did I already know this, and he had no honest answer for it yet, only the growing, uncomfortable certainty that an answer existed somewhere just beyond what his own memory was willing to surrender.

✦

He searched the anvil's surface properly this time, working outward from the exact spot where his palms had rested, rather than the base and surrounding stone he'd already combed bare hours earlier. The inscription revealed itself only when he cleared away a fine layer of ash that had settled, undisturbed, into a shallow groove worn into the metal — letters in a script his eyes recognized from the old ethnography's footnote, faint but legible once the ash lifted clear.

I am a verdict given not with anger, but with joy. I cannot be claimed by wrath, only by laughter.

He read it three times, his fingers tracing the worn grooves of each character as though touch might confirm what his eyes were already certain of. It matched nothing in his memory of the spear's dark, wordless cruelty. It matched everything in the vision's raw, undirected delight. He pulled out the small notebook he'd carried since the archive, hands not quite steady, and copied the phrase down twice, once in the original script as best his untrained hand could render it, once in his own translation, unwilling to trust a discovery this fragile to memory alone.

✦

He sat back on his heels and let the full weight of the realization settle into him properly. He had been thinking of this search — of the sword, of judgment itself — entirely wrong from the start. He had approached it the way a soldier approaches a weapon: something built for war, something that carried grief because grief was simply the tax war always levied on everyone who touched it. The inscription suggested something else entirely. A verdict that could not be claimed by wrath. A judgment that answered cruelty not with more cruelty but with something closer to relief.

He thought of the spear from his dreams, of the specific, patient malice in every judgment it delivered, and found himself circling a conclusion he had no way yet to confirm: that the two weapons might not be simply different tools built for the same grim purpose, but something closer to opposites — two answers to the same question, arriving at conclusions that could hardly have been further apart. He held the thought loosely, unwilling to trust it as settled fact on the strength of one inscription and one vision, however certain it felt sitting here in the dark.

It raised a question he had no comfortable place to set down. If one weapon answered cruelty with more cruelty, and the other answered it with something closer to mercy, what did it mean that his own dreams had only ever shown him the first, and never once, not in all these months, offered him a glimpse of the second? He had assumed, without ever quite examining the assumption, that whatever haunted his sleep was the whole of the story. Sitting here with the inscription's plain, contradictory claim in front of him, he understood for the first time that he might have only ever been shown half of it.

✦

The boat waited exactly where the fisherman had promised it would, though the sun had fully set by the time Mikel finally stumbled back down to the shore, breathless from a scramble through undergrowth he could no longer properly see. He had half expected to find the shallows empty, the promise from that morning quietly revoked the way promises so often were once the cost of keeping them became real. The fisherman only glanced once at his face, taking in whatever the last few hours had left written there, and said nothing about the lateness at all — only pushed off from the shallows the moment Mikel climbed aboard, his oars finding their rhythm in the near-total dark with the ease of someone who had made this crossing blind more than once before.

"Last time I brought someone here," the fisherman said, several long strokes into the silence, "they didn't come back the same either."

Mikel found, to his own surprise, that he had courage enough tonight to push the question further than he had on the way in. "Did they come back better, or worse?"

The fisherman took a full stroke of the oars before he answered, the boat gliding forward into darkness that gave no hint of the mainland still somewhere ahead of them. "Came back with a new question instead of an answer," he said finally. "Same as you, I'd guess."

"What was theirs?" Mikel asked, though some part of him already suspected he wasn't going to get an answer worth the asking.

"Not mine to tell." The fisherman's voice carried no unkindness in the refusal, only the flat, settled finality of a man who sounded, to Mikel's ear, like someone who had made his peace with a boundary a long time ago and had no intention of revisiting it tonight. "Same as yours won't be mine to tell, once you've had time to sit with it properly." He said nothing further, and Mikel understood, watching the set of the man's shoulders in the dark, that nothing further was coming tonight regardless of how he asked for it.

He let the silence stand, and let his hand find the notebook still tucked against his ribs, the inscription's careful copy safe inside it, and sat with his own new question the rest of the long crossing home. The fog thinned as they went, the mainland's scattered lights finally resolving somewhere ahead of the bow, and Mikel found himself, for the first time in longer than he could easily place, less afraid of what he didn't yet understand than curious about it — a small, unfamiliar shift, and one he suspected, turning the inscription's words over once more in his mind, he owed to whatever had laughed at him from inside that vision, whoever or whatever it actually turned out to be.

Chapter 23

A Thief's Legacy

The trail Sariel followed deeper into the Tower had nothing to do with sight or sound. It was ambition he tracked now, the particular psychic residue a mind as hungry as Belial's left behind wherever it lingered too long over a text — a mark on the archive's accumulated silence, distinct from the ambient screaming the way a single voice stands apart from a crowd. He had learned, across centuries of hunting things that didn't want to be found, to read exactly this kind of residue, and Belial's had settled into this place deeply enough that even months later it remained plain to a mind trained to look for it.

The trail ended at a hidden study three levels below the one where the vision had found him, a chamber concealed behind a shelf that swung inward at a touch, its hinge worn smooth by a use its builders had clearly never intended anyone else to discover. Sariel stepped through into a room untouched by the archive's general chaos, shelves here arranged with the particular, obsessive order of a mind that had wanted nothing left to chance.

The screaming hush that filled the rest of the Tower faded almost entirely once the shelf sealed shut behind him, and the quiet that replaced it felt, if anything, more unsettling than the din he'd left behind — a silence too deliberate to be accidental, the kind of quiet a room earns only when someone has gone to considerable trouble to keep it that way. He moved through it carefully, still favoring the side where the vision's aftermath had left a dull, persistent ache behind his ribs, cataloguing the room's contents with the same patient thoroughness he brought to every space he'd ever needed to search without leaving evidence that he'd searched it.

✦

The fragments of the Codex Mortalis sat displayed across a reading table exactly where Belial had apparently left them, undisturbed since whatever night he'd last studied them here. Sariel approached slowly, reading what remained of the ancient text with the careful, hungry attention of a man who understood, better than most, exactly how much a single surviving fragment could sometimes cost to obtain. The pages spoke only in fragments and riddles of weapons neither Heaven nor Hell had ever fully claimed as their own creation, offering nothing so plain as an origin, only the persistent, unsettling suggestion that one existed somewhere beneath the text's careful evasions.

Belial's own annotations crowded the margins in a hand grown increasingly urgent as the pages progressed, question after question scrawled beside passages the original text answered only in riddles. Sariel had expected arrogance in those margins. He found instead something closer to desperation — a mind working frantically to understand a power it had already decided to seize, regardless of whether understanding ever actually arrived in time to matter.

He traced the progression across a dozen fragments, watching the handwriting itself change as the study wore on — confident at first, almost dismissive in its brevity, then gradually cramped and urgent, entire pages given over to the same question rephrased a dozen different ways. Whatever Belial had been searching for in these pages, he had clearly not found it cleanly. He had found enough to act on, and had acted, and Sariel understood, reading the last and most frantic of the annotations, that acting without fully understanding was very likely the reason Belial now carried a permanent, unwanted cost he had never once anticipated when he'd first set out to claim his prize.

✦

It was the different hand that caught his attention properly — older, more cautious, its ink faded to a shade Belial's fresher annotations hadn't yet begun to match. It sat crowded into a corner Belial's own notes had left untouched, as though whoever wrote it had known enough to leave a warning where a careless reader might miss it, and a careful one wouldn't.

Whoever claims this without being its rightful owner will find no glory. They will find a debt.

Sariel copied the line into memory with the same careful precision he gave everything worth remembering, and became aware, in the same instant, that he was no longer alone in the chamber.

"You've found the old margin note." The voice belonged to a minor archivist, elderly even by the standards of a realm that measured age differently than mortals did, his robes marking him as one of the Tower's permanent staff rather than a visitor passing through. He'd entered without Sariel noticing, and stood now examining the reading table with what struck Sariel as the mild, proprietary interest of a man checking on furniture rather than a man guarding forbidden knowledge. "Most who come through here miss it entirely. Your lord's attention rarely wandered that far from the text itself."

Sariel kept his borrowed indifference carefully in place. "Who else has consulted this collection, before my lord?"

"Before Lord Belial?" The archivist gave a short, dry laugh, entirely unguarded — a laugh that read, to Sariel, like a man too bored with his own work to suspect the question carried any real weight behind it. "No one, in centuries. This isn't the kind of material anyone reads twice for pleasure. It gets read twice because the first reading never actually made sense."

"And the older hand," Sariel asked, careful to let the question trail after the first as though it barely mattered. "Do the Tower's records say whose it was?"

"No name attached to it that I've ever found, and I've looked, if only out of my own idle curiosity over the years." The archivist considered the question with more genuine interest than he'd shown the rest of the exchange. "Older than the collection itself, if the ink's any judge. Whoever wrote it wasn't cataloguing anything. They were warning off whoever came after them, and didn't much care whether anyone ever read it." He shrugged, already losing interest again, and drifted back toward the doorway without waiting for Sariel to respond further. "Take your time. No one's come looking for this dust in longer than either of us has probably been alive."

Sariel said nothing more, and let the archivist go, filing the answer away as carefully as he'd filed the margin note itself.

✦

The records of the heist sat further back in the study, in a locked drawer Belial's own annotations had clearly indicated the location of without ever bothering to properly conceal, arrogance winning out, in the end, over caution even here. Sariel read through them with the same flat, professional attention he'd have given any hunt's final piece of evidence, and felt something cold settle into his chest as the full shape of it assembled itself in front of him.

The plan itself had been built in careful stages, each one recorded with the dispassionate precision of a man documenting a campaign rather than confessing to a theft — a false treaty offered to buy passage past the Sepulcher's outer wards, a manufactured rivalry set to draw its lesser guardians away from their posts at the exact hour Belial needed them absent, and finally the sacrifice itself, timed to the precise moment the deception could no longer hold on its own. Nothing in the notes read like improvisation. Every step had been mapped in advance, contingencies included, the whole operation assembled with the same patient thoroughness Sariel recognized from his own centuries of careful hunts.

Belial had not simply found Lament through diligence or divine favor. He had orchestrated its theft, deliberately and at real cost — the sacrifice of Aamon, Marshal of the Infernal Legions, offered up to whatever guardian had once protected the Weeping Sepulcher's approach. The notes named the Sepulcher's location plainly, along with the exact sequence of deceptions Belial had used to breach its final ward, none of it recorded with any pride in craftsmanship, only the flat, transactional satisfaction of a thief cataloguing a job well executed.

Sariel read the account of Aamon's sacrifice twice, the second time more slowly than the first. Belial's notes gave a commander of Aamon's standing exactly three lines — no mention of the legions he'd marshaled, no acknowledgment of the rank he'd spent centuries building, only the bare fact of the sacrifice and a brief, clinical note on how effectively it had opened the Sepulcher's final approach. Sariel had killed a great many things in his long service, and had rarely troubled himself afterward over what any of them had once commanded before he ended them. He found, reading Belial's cold three lines about a marshal reduced to a single tactical expenditure, that this particular death bothered him more than most of his own ever had.

✦

Sariel sat back from the drawer's contents and let the larger truth of it settle properly into place. He had come here half expecting to find evidence of some terrible, singular genius — a mastermind who had earned his prize through understanding no one else possessed. He found instead a thief, patient and ruthless but fundamentally ordinary in his methods, who had simply been willing to spend what others weren't to take something that had never once belonged to him.

That understanding reframed everything Gabriel had sent him here to learn. The weapons were not artifacts of either side's own making, prizes any general might have legitimately claimed through conquest or divine right. They belonged to neither Heaven nor Hell at all. Belial had not won Lament. He had stolen it, the same way any common thief stole anything too valuable to walk away from honestly, and the war both realms had spent centuries fighting over its use had never once involved a party who actually owned what was being fought over.

He thought of Gabriel's suspicion in the cold observatory, the careful, unfinished shape of it — that whatever had killed Malthus predated even Hell's own knowledge of itself, that a trail worth finding waited somewhere in this very Tower. Gabriel had guessed at the size of the mystery without yet guessing at its nature. Sariel now carried both, and understood, sitting alone in a dead man's study three levels beneath Hell's oldest archive, that some truths cost considerably more to carry than they had ever seemed to cost simply to seek.

✦

Sariel took nothing else from the study — no fragment pocketed, no page torn free, nothing that might mark him as having been here at all should the study ever be searched again. But before he stepped back through the concealed shelf, he paused a final moment over the spot where the unsigned margin note had waited, faded and easy to overlook, for however many silent centuries had passed since someone first thought to write it.

Gabriel had not asked him to remember a warning meant for thieves. Nothing in his instructions had mentioned it at all. But some old instinct, the same patient certainty that had made him good at tracking souls too well hidden for anyone else to find, told him plainly that the warning would matter again before this was finished — that a debt promised to whoever claimed something without rightful ownership was not the kind of detail a hunter of his experience could afford to trust to memory alone. He drew a small scrap of blank parchment from the lining of his forged papers, where it had been folded flat and unnoticed since he'd crossed into Hell, and copied the line onto it in his own careful hand, letter for letter, before folding it away again somewhere no search of a records clerk would think to look.

It was, he understood, the first decision he had made on this entire mission that Gabriel had not specifically authorized. He found he did not regret it. A hunter who only ever followed instructions to the letter caught nothing that hadn't already been named for him in advance, and Sariel had built a long, quiet career on being considerably better than that.

Then he stepped back through the shelf, sealed it behind him with the same careful patience he'd used to open it, and turned his attention toward the long, dangerous walk back out of Hell's oldest and hungriest archive.

Chapter 24

The Path of Laughter

The long drive back from the coast felt shorter than the journey out had, though Mikel knew well enough that the distance hadn't changed — only the shape of what he was carrying home with him. He kept the notebook on the passenger seat within easy reach the entire way, the inscription's careful copy safe inside it, and found himself, more than once, glancing over at it at red lights the way a man checks on something he isn't yet convinced will still be there the next time he looks.

He had left the mainland searching for a weapon. He returned searching for something he had no proper name for yet, only a shape borrowed from an anvil's worn inscription — I cannot be claimed by wrath, only by laughter. It was not a smaller question than the one he'd left with. If anything, it felt considerably larger, an entire register of human and divine experience he had spent his whole career treating as beneath serious historical inquiry, now sitting squarely at the center of everything he needed to understand.

He thought of every lecture he'd ever given on the mechanics of judgment in the ancient world — verdicts, punishments, the careful architecture societies built to decide who deserved what. He had never once, in nine years of teaching, devoted a single class session to joy as a serious historical category, the same way no serious military historian devoted a lecture to laughter on a battlefield. It had never occurred to him that the omission itself might be the mistake, that an entire register of human experience could simply be filed away as unserious by scholars too committed to gravity to notice what they'd left out.

✦

He returned to his university office two days later and found it exactly as he'd left it, dust settled thin across a desk he'd abandoned mid-thought weeks ago, and began, almost immediately, tearing his own research apart and rebuilding it from an angle he'd never once considered legitimate before Turein. He abandoned the military history entirely, at least for now, and opened a search that would have looked, to any colleague glancing over his shoulder, like the beginning of a complete professional collapse — trickster gods, holy fools, laughing buddhas, mercurial smiths who forged with mischief instead of gravity. He cross-referenced traditions that had never once been placed in conversation with each other in any serious academic context he could find, searching for the single thread that might connect divinity, creation, and unguarded joy.

The work consumed him in a way even the sword's darker mythology never had. He caught himself, more than once, forgetting entire meals, surfacing from an afternoon's reading only when the light through his office window had shifted enough to startle him into checking the time. It was obsessive in a way he recognized, distantly, as unhealthy, and he found he no longer had much interest in stopping simply because it was unhealthy.

He built a working list on the back page of his notebook, adding to it every evening as the day's reading yielded some new fragment worth keeping — a coyote spirit who taught by breaking rules rather than enforcing them, a laughing buddha whose belly held the weight of every burden his followers set down in his presence, a mercurial smith from a tradition three continents removed from Turein's own who forged, according to the surviving fragments, "with a hammer that never once struck out of anger." The pattern held wherever he looked for it, faint but unmistakable, connecting traditions that had never once been asked to speak to each other before. He filled four pages with names and fragments before the week was through, and understood, reading back over the list, that he was no longer chasing a single myth. Whether these scattered, unrelated figures were echoes of one single thing or simply humanity reaching for the same comfort by a dozen separate roads, he wasn't yet prepared to say. The list alone was enough to keep him reading.

✦

The archivist found him there on the fourth evening, arriving with what Mikel took to be the pretense of returning a book he hadn't actually requested, and stood in his doorway a long moment before saying what she seemed to have come to say.

"This isn't comparative folklore anymore, Ardon." She said it without her usual dry humor, her curiosity plain enough now that she'd stopped bothering to disguise it as professional interest. "This is an obsession with one very specific thing. Are you ever going to tell me what it actually is?"

Mikel looked up from the sprawl of open books across his desk, and found, for the first time in this entire strange and lengthening ordeal, that he didn't immediately reach for a deflection. "I don't know how to explain it yet myself," he said, "without it sounding like I need a different kind of help than a librarian can give me. Give me a little longer to be sure before I try."

She laughed at that, but carefully, the particular laugh of someone who has heard the honesty underneath a joke and isn't quite sure yet what to do with it. "When you do know," she said, "you already know where to find me." She didn't press further. Instead she set the book she'd brought down on the corner of his desk, spine facing him, and tapped it once. "This is the third time this month you've had me pulling cross-references on local folklore collections nobody's touched in decades. There's an uncatalogued regional set in the sub-basement, filed under 'miscellaneous,' that's never made it into the digital system at all. If whatever you're chasing has roots anywhere near here, it's more likely to be sitting down there than in anything you'll find searching properly indexed material." She left him to his sprawling, obsessive research without another word about it, the book's spine still facing him from the corner of the desk.

He sat with the exchange a while after she'd gone, dwelling on the strange, unfamiliar lightness of having answered a question honestly instead of simply managing it away. It was a small thing, in the end. It did not feel small at all.

✦

The trail that finally moved him forward arrived exactly where she'd told him to look. He spent two full days working through the sub-basement's uncatalogued shelves before he found it, a slim collection of local legends filed under a heading he'd walked past a hundred times without ever once stopping to read it properly: Folktales of the Navarion Valley.

The stories inside described a wandering musician who appeared, in one guise or another, throughout centuries of local record — a figure whose laughter, the tales insisted, could mend what grief had broken, whose songs carried a strange power to coax life back into fields that should have long since failed. One account, dated nearly a century back, described a farmer who had lost his entire harvest to blight, and who swore afterward that a stranger with a guitar had sat with him one whole night on the ruined field, playing nothing in particular, until something in the man's chest simply stopped aching long enough for him to sleep. Another, older still, spoke of a plague year in which the sick who heard the musician's songs through their fevers reported dreams free of the terror the rest of the town endured nightly. The academic tradition had filed him away as a beloved local quirk, a piece of regional color no serious folklorist had ever thought worth cross-referencing against anything larger.

Mikel read every surviving fragment twice, his pulse climbing steadily with each new detail, and found himself circling a single, stunned realization he didn't yet have the nerve to say aloud. Every account agreed on one detail without exception: the musician never once appeared angry, no matter how dark the circumstance that had drawn him. He wondered, turning the last fragment over in his hands, whether he had finally found the same laughing, ungovernable presence the inscription on Turein had promised him existed — not a myth confined to one impossible island, but something considerably larger, and considerably closer, than he had let himself imagine.

✦

The collection had been sitting, uncatalogued in any meaningful sense, inside his own university's archive the entire time. He sat back from the desk, the full weight of that specific coincidence settling into him slowly, and thought of Turein — the ancient forge, the impossible sea crossing, the whole mythic weight of a search he'd assumed could only be answered somewhere far from anything as mundane as his own daily walk to work.

He had always kept the two halves of his life carefully separate in his own mind: the ordinary scholar who lectured on medieval sieges, and the man haunted by dreams he couldn't place, chasing a mystery that felt, by its very nature, like it belonged somewhere else entirely. Sitting here now with the Navarion folktales spread open in front of him, he understood, for the first time, that the separation might not be as clean as he'd always assumed. This particular thread, at least, hadn't required an ocean crossing to find. It had been sitting in his own archive, filed under a heading he'd walked past a hundred times.

He sat with that thought long enough for the office around him to grow properly dark, the campus outside his window settling into the particular hush it took on once the evening classes let out and the last stragglers finally drifted toward home. He had spent a small fortune and no small measure of his own safety crossing an ocean to stand in front of a cold forge on an island that barely wanted to be found. He wondered, not without a certain bleak humor, how much more of this search might turn out to be closer to home than he'd assumed, and how much of it would still demand he go looking considerably farther before it was through. He didn't yet know which this local thread would turn out to be. He only knew it was worth chasing further before he decided.

✦

He worked later than he meant to that night, and left the archive only once the building's other lights had already gone dark around him, the folktales' scattered fragments still occupying most of his attention as he crossed the quiet campus toward the faculty parking beyond it. The night had turned cool enough that his breath showed faintly under the walkway lamps, and the campus at this hour belonged almost entirely to him, the ordinary daytime bustle stripped away to leave only empty benches and the distant hum of traffic somewhere beyond the gates.

A street musician had set up near the main gate anyway, guitar case open in front of him, playing something low and unhurried to an audience of essentially no one at this hour. Mikel had passed buskers at that same gate a hundred times before without giving any of them a second thought, and tonight was, on its surface, no different from any of the others.

Mikel walked past without truly registering him at all, too deep in his own thoughts to spare more than the vague, unconscious awareness a man gives to background noise. He did not see the musician's fingers still on the strings for one full second as he passed, the song catching and holding on a single unresolved note before picking back up exactly where it had left off. He did not see the look that crossed the musician's face either, gone again almost before it had fully arrived — unreadable, brief, and entirely wasted on a man who never once turned to notice it.

Mikel reached his car without incident, and drove home with the Navarion folktales still occupying most of his mind, giving the musician at the gate no further thought at all once he'd passed him.

Chapter 25

The Hunter and the Hunted

Arin had not slept in the same city twice since the Grey City, and he'd stopped expecting to anytime soon. He moved the way he'd once moved between contracts, city to city, cash to stolen cash, except now the men reading his movements weren't rival mercenaries or the occasional police sketch. He could feel them if he held still long enough to pay proper attention — demons circling in hungry, brutish probes that never quite closed the distance, and something else behind them, colder and more patient, watching from a remove he couldn't quite locate no matter how carefully he searched for it. He trusted neither. He had learned, across a month that felt considerably longer than a month, that trusting either side of this war was the fastest way to end up owned by it.

He kept moving because moving was the only strategy he had left that still worked reliably. A man who never slept in the same place twice was harder to corner than a man who thought he'd found somewhere safe.

He had grown fluent, over these last weeks, in a kind of exhaustion he'd never once encountered across a career built on considerably more conventional dangers. Contract work had taught him to read a room, a target, an exit. It had never once taught him to read the particular pressure of being watched by two entire realms at once, each one weighing him against a purpose he still didn't fully understand himself. He ate when food presented itself, slept in short, careful stretches that never let his guard drop all the way, and kept walking, because the alternative to walking was standing still long enough for one of his watchers to finally decide he was worth the trouble of closing the distance.

✦

He shared a table without meaning to in a refugee shelter two cities south of the Grey City's ruins, one of the countless makeshift kitchens that had sprung up in the weeks since the tower's collapse to feed people who'd fled a city that had, by every official account, simply suffered an industrial accident. The shelter itself was a converted gymnasium, cots lined in rows too close together, the particular hum of too many displaced lives crowded into too little space filling every corner of it. Arin had chosen this table specifically because it sat farthest from the door, the seat that let him watch every entrance without appearing to watch anything at all. The man across from him hadn't stopped talking about it since Arin sat down, the particular compulsive retelling of someone who needed to keep saying a thing out loud until it finally stopped feeling unreal.

"You hear what really happened at that facility?" The man leaned in, voice dropped to the pitch people used for things they weren't supposed to believe. "My cousin worked security two buildings over. Says something walked in there and just — undid people. Not killed. Undid. Like they were never solid to begin with." He studied Arin a moment, searching his face for the reaction he'd apparently been fishing for from everyone at this table for days. "Some people are saying it was an angel. You hear that too?"

Arin held the man's gaze, weighing the lie against the truth and finding, as he usually did lately, that the honest answer cost him less than either extreme. "I heard it was something that shouldn't have been able to happen at all," he said. "Everything past that is just words people need so they can sleep at night."

The man studied him a moment longer, something in his expression settling rather than sharpening, as if the answer had satisfied him more thoroughly than a clean confirmation would have. He nodded slowly, and went back to his food without pressing further, and Arin sat with the strange, hollow feeling of having just watched a stranger accept a version of his own legend that no longer had anything to do with the man who'd actually lived it.

He finished his meal in the silence that followed, listening to the rest of the room's low murmur of half-formed rumor drifting around him — someone else's cousin, someone else's version of the same impossible night, each retelling drifting a little further from whatever had actually happened in that corridor. He understood, sitting there, that the story no longer belonged to him at all. It belonged to this room now, to every shelter like it scattered across however many cities the Grey City's refugees had fled to, growing stranger and larger with each new telling, and there was nothing left for him to do about that except let it happen.

✦

He found the demon three days later, in a warehouse district gone half to rust on the outskirts of a city he'd already half-forgotten the name of, tracking a scent of absence that had grown familiar enough over the past month to follow almost without conscious thought. The creature had chosen its hunting ground carelessly, close enough to a residential block that Arin had spent an hour simply confirming it hadn't already taken anyone before he moved. It hadn't. He counted that a small mercy, and let it buy the creature nothing once he started closing the distance between them.

He'd nearly had the wrong read on it entirely. The demon broke for the open street the moment it caught his scent instead of the other way around, faster and more coordinated than the clumsy scavengers he'd grown used to running down across the past month, and for one disorienting stretch of alley Arin understood, with a clarity that cost him a full second of hesitation, that this particular hunt had briefly reversed itself without his permission. It doubled back through a gap in a collapsed fence he hadn't accounted for, nearly putting a full block of open ground between them before he caught the shift and cut across a loading yard to head it off instead of simply chasing it. He reached the rusted dock at the district's dead end half a step ahead of it, close enough that the demon's own momentum carried it straight into his waiting grip. It hadn't been hunting anyone tonight. It had simply, for one brief and genuinely uncomfortable stretch of alley, very nearly stopped being the one being hunted.

The interrogation that followed bore no resemblance to conversation. He held the creature's essence gathered loosely in one closed fist, the threat of unmaking it plain and unhurried, and let the demon's own fear do most of the remaining work.

"The Grey City facility," Arin said, "was not the only one."

"No." The demon's voice came out thin, flickering at the edges the way its borrowed shape kept flickering under the pressure Arin held it with. "There are others. All of them feeding the same operation. One central collection point, one portal, everything else just — tributary."

"Where."

The demon hesitated, and Arin tightened his grip fractionally, enough to make the cost of hesitation plain without needing to spend more than that. "Hidden," the demon said, the word coming apart into something closer to a gurgle. "Shielded, in a place with enough ambient psychic noise to mask the signature from anyone not already looking directly at it. An old university. Human place of knowledge, human place of power, more of both than you'd expect from somewhere that size." It gave the name like a man surrendering the last coin he had left to spend. "Navarion."

✦

Arin let the name settle into him the way he'd learned to let every important piece of information settle before acting on it, and felt something sharpen in his chest that had nothing to do with the compass's usual pull toward absence. This was not simply another harvesting site to shut down. This was the center of the entire operation, the place every tributary in this monstrous network ultimately fed back into, and he understood, holding the demon's fear in one closed hand, that finding it changed the shape of everything he'd been doing since the Grey City.

He thought of every silent captive he'd passed in that first facility, of the woman from the fourth cell and the boy in the extraction frame and the countless others he'd never had the chance to reach at all. If this university truly sat at the center of the whole operation, then every one of those harvesting sites, every quiet horror scattered across however many cities this network actually spanned, all led back to a single point on a map he now finally had a name for. That was not a small thing to hold. He held it anyway, the way he'd learned to hold everything else this last month had handed him.

He was no longer running from something. He had, for the first time since this whole nightmare had started pulling him along behind it, an actual destination — a place to go toward instead of simply somewhere further to flee.

✦

He should have ended it there, the interrogation complete, the information extracted and confirmed. He found himself instead studying the demon's face a moment longer, some old, unexamined curiosity surfacing before he'd fully decided to let it. "Do you recognize anything in me," he asked, "beyond what I can do to you right now?"

The demon's eyes, wide with fear a moment before, narrowed instead into something closer to genuine confusion. "Recognize?" It searched his face the way a man searches a stranger's for a resemblance he can't quite place, and came up with nothing solid enough to answer. "You're strong. Stronger than anything wearing that shape should be. I don't know what that means beyond what it plainly is." It shook its head, some of the fear returning now that the strange question had passed. "Should it mean something else?"

Arin didn't answer. He released the creature's essence and let it flee into the dark without pursuit, and stood alone a long moment in the rusting warehouse, turning the question over the way he'd once turned over a veteran's strange remark in a bar he could no longer picture clearly — you ever get the feeling you've done this before. He had no better answer for it now than he'd had then. He only knew that the question kept returning at exactly the moments he most needed to stay focused on something simpler, and that its persistence was, in its own quiet way, becoming a kind of answer all its own, even if he couldn't yet read what it actually said.

He turned the strangeness of it over a while longer, standing alone in the warehouse's rusted quiet, and found himself wondering, not for the first time, why a captured demon's blank confusion should matter to him at all. He had asked plenty of frightened creatures plenty of questions across the last month, and had never once cared, before tonight, whether any of them looked back at him and saw something they recognized. He made himself stop turning it over before it cost him more focus than he could afford to spend on it tonight.

He turned north, toward Navarion, and let the question stay unanswered, the same way he'd learned to let everything else about this last month stay unanswered. The road ahead would take days regardless of how fast he moved, and he found himself, walking away from the warehouse's rusted quiet, oddly grateful for the distance. It would give him time to think of nothing in particular, which was, these days, the closest thing to rest he still knew how to find.

Chapter 26

Report from the Abyss

Sariel timed his return the way he timed everything that mattered, waiting until the shift change put the thinnest possible stretch of Uriel's patrols between himself and the observatory's private stair. He still had to cross two open courtyards he would once have crossed without a second thought, and found, doing it now, that his usual economy of movement had gone slightly, stubbornly wrong — a half-second's lag between decision and motion that hadn't been there before the Tower, a weight behind his ribs that made the climb up the final flight of stairs cost him more than it should have.

He counted the crossing in smaller increments than he usually allowed himself, one courtyard at a time, one patrol interval at a time, refusing to let the growing distance to the observatory become a single overwhelming span he'd have to cross all at once. It was a trick he'd learned centuries ago, on hunts that ran longer than his body wanted to carry him, and he was faintly, distantly aware that needing it tonight, for a walk this short, told him something about his own condition he'd rather not have had to learn this way. He reached the observatory's door on legs that held, which was, he told himself, all he'd actually needed of them tonight.

Gabriel was already waiting when he entered, and whatever composure Gabriel usually kept in place for council sessions had clearly not survived the sight of him. "You're hurt."

"I'm here," Sariel said, which was not quite the same claim, and both of them understood the distinction well enough that neither bothered pointing it out.

Gabriel studied him a moment longer before stepping aside to let him properly into the room, taking in the details Sariel hadn't bothered trying to hide — the faint, dried line of blood at one nostril, the stiffness in how he carried his own weight, the particular grey exhaustion that no amount of controlled composure could fully disguise from someone who'd known him this long. "Sit," Gabriel said, gesturing to the chair beside the star-map rather than his usual place standing before it. "Whatever you've brought back can wait the extra minute it takes you to actually sit down for it."

Sariel didn't argue. That alone, more than anything he might have said aloud, told Gabriel plainly enough how much the crossing back had cost him.

✦

He gave his report the way he gave every report, flat and exact, the emotional weight of it carried entirely in the facts rather than in how he chose to deliver them. He described the Tower's screaming archive first, the hidden study behind its concealed shelf, the annotations that grew more desperate the deeper Belial's obsession had carried him into the Codex Mortalis. Belial had not forged Lament, had not earned it through any craft or divine favor either side of this war might have recognized as legitimate. He had stolen it — planned the theft with the same patient, transactional precision he brought to everything, sacrificed Aamon, Marshal of the Infernal Legions, to buy the final approach, and walked away from the Weeping Sepulcher with a weapon that had never once belonged to Heaven, Hell, or anyone still living to claim it honestly.

He set the details out in the order he'd found them, refusing to let his own exhaustion compress the account into something less complete than it needed to be. Gabriel deserved the whole shape of it, not a summary shaped by how tired the telling made him. He drew a small, folded scrap of parchment from where he'd kept it hidden since the Tower and set it on the table between them — the unsigned warning he'd copied by hand beside Belial's own frantic notes, in his own careful script, the debt it promised anyone who claimed ownership they didn't rightfully hold. He watched Gabriel's expression sharpen at the sight of it in a way it hadn't sharpened at any of the rest.

"The Codex Mortalis names it plainly enough, in its own way," Sariel said. "The weapons are relics of an age that predates the war we're fighting now. Neither side made them. Neither side has ever had the right to them, whatever either realm has told itself these past centuries to justify the fighting."

Gabriel was quiet a long moment, the particular quiet of a man receiving confirmation of something he'd feared without yet knowing how large the fear actually was. "Then the war we've spent centuries treating as sacred," he said finally, "was never anything more than two sides squabbling over stolen property neither of them understood." He said it without theater, without the raised voice the claim might have earned from someone less exhausted by the shape of what it actually meant, and the flatness of it landed harder in the quiet room than any dramatic declaration could have managed.

He turned away from Sariel for a moment, one hand resting against the edge of the star-map's frame, and Sariel watched him stand with it in silence, saying nothing further for long enough that the silence itself became its own kind of answer. Sariel had delivered difficult reports before, to commanders who absorbed them and moved immediately to the next order. He had never once watched a report cost someone quite this much before they'd even finished deciding what to do with it.

✦

Raphael had said nothing since Sariel entered, and Iofiel, standing near the star-map with the same intent, cautious attention she gave everything that threatened to become a crisis, was already turning the implications over aloud — the cycle, the sword, the pattern she'd been circling since the council first argued over a lost commander. "If the weapons predate the war," she said, mostly to herself, working through it out loud the way she always did when a theory finally found solid ground to stand on, "then everything we've built our understanding of this conflict around has been resting on a foundation neither side actually laid. The cycle's own instability, the way it let something in that signature from the Grey City resist forgetting completely — none of that fits comfortably inside a war that's only ever been Heaven against Hell. It fits considerably better inside something older."

Sariel let her voice wash past him without fully tracking every word of it, too tired to hold an unfolding argument together the way he ordinarily would have. He assumed, without examining the assumption closely, that the feeling was simply exhaustion catching up with him at last.

Raphael crossed the room instead of joining the debate, and knelt in front of Sariel's chair with the particular, focused attention of a healer who had stopped listening to the room the moment something in front of him demanded closer study. "Something is still attached to you," he said, quiet enough that it took Sariel a moment to realize the words were meant for him specifically and not for the wider conversation. "Not a wound. Something that came back with you from the Tower."

Sariel hadn't mentioned the vision beyond its bare outline. He hadn't thought there was anything further worth mentioning. "I felt it happen," he admitted. "I didn't realize it had followed me out."

✦

Raphael pressed Veritas flat against his own chest rather than reaching toward Sariel directly, his eyes half-closed, his expression carrying a concentration Sariel had never once seen him wear in all their years of shared service. The staff's light dimmed rather than brightened, drawing inward instead of radiating out the way Sariel had always seen it work in every healing he'd ever watched Raphael perform. "It's alive, in the small way residue like this is alive," Raphael said, mostly to himself. "Still trying to work its way further in. Given enough time, it would have finished what the vision started, and I don't think either of us would have liked what finished looked like." His hand, closed around the staff's haft, had begun to tremble, faint but unmistakable.

"What are you doing?" Iofiel had crossed the room too, her earlier theorizing forgotten entirely in the face of whatever she was now watching unfold.

"Something I don't do," Raphael said, not looking away from the staff. "Veritas doesn't only heal. It can hold a thing in place instead of dissolving it — the same function that could, if I let it, imprison a soul rather than simply mend one. I've never once used it that way in front of anyone. I don't like this part of what I can do." His hand tightened further, the tremor deepening rather than easing. "But leaving it inside him would be worse."

✦

The residue came free of Sariel in a way he felt more than saw, a cold, foreign pressure lifting from somewhere behind his sternum, gone so completely and so suddenly that its absence felt, for one disorienting moment, like a wound in its own right. Raphael held it a long moment, visible now as a faint, writhing distortion caught somewhere between the staff's head and his own closed fist, before setting it down on the table with the exact, careful economy of a man setting down something he did not entirely trust to stay where he'd put it.

"Have you ever done that to something with a mind of its own," Iofiel asked, "not just a residue like this?"

Raphael didn't answer directly. "I hope I never have to find out what that feels like," he said, and left the inert fragment sitting sealed on the table between them, its faint distortion gone still now rather than fading, contained rather than dissolved. He said nothing further, his own stillness carrying none of the satisfaction Sariel would have expected from a task completed well.

Sariel touched the spot on his own chest where the pressure had sat a moment before, and felt nothing there at all — no ache, no absence, no evidence it had ever existed. That, more than any lingering pain could have, unsettled him. A wound he could measure. This left him with only Raphael's word that anything had been there to begin with, and a hunter's old, uneasy instinct that the things he couldn't measure had always been the ones worth worrying about most.

✦

Gabriel let the room settle before he spoke again, and when he did, his voice carried the particular weight of a man reorganizing an entire understanding of the war he'd spent his existence serving. "If the weapons belong to neither side," he said, "then Miguel's soul is not the only anomaly this council needs to find. Whatever forged those weapons predates us. If it left one mark on this war, it likely left others." He looked to Iofiel, then to Raphael, weighing something Sariel couldn't fully follow in his current state. "We are no longer simply searching for a lost commander. We are searching for whatever else that older power touched, and we need to understand it before Hell finishes understanding it first."

"The two signals," Iofiel said. "The one near Navarion, the one in the Grey City. If they're connected to this at all—"

"Then they matter considerably more than a council divided over a sword ever accounted for," Gabriel finished. He crossed to the star-map, studying the two familiar points of light with an attention that carried more weight now than it had the first time he'd stood before them. "We've been treating this as a search for one lost commander and one dangerous anomaly, two separate problems that happened to share a season. If Sariel's right about what he found, they may be pieces of the same much older question, and I have no intention of continuing to treat them as though they aren't until something forces the correction on us."

Iofiel moved to stand beside him at the map, her own attention already sharpening toward the practical shape of the problem rather than lingering on the theory behind it. "Then we watch both signals properly this time. Not from a distance, and not through secondhand reports months after the fact. If either one shifts again the way the Grey City did, I want this council to know within the hour, not to learn about it from a rumor that's already traveled halfway across a continent by the time it reaches us."

"Agreed," Gabriel said. He turned back to Sariel, something apologetic entering his expression for the first time since Sariel had arrived. "You've given this council more in one night than months of careful debate managed on its own."

✦

"Do you want me to return to the Tower," Sariel asked, because it needed asking plainly, and because some part of him had already begun bracing for the answer before Gabriel gave it.

"No." Gabriel said it without hesitation, without the diplomatic softening he sometimes wrapped around difficult orders. "You've paid enough for this phase of it. What comes next isn't infiltration. It's patience — watching, waiting, letting the two signals we already have tell us more before we risk anyone else the way I risked you." He studied Sariel a moment longer, seeming to search for some sign of relief that didn't fully arrive. "That should sound like good news."

"It does," Sariel said, and meant it, mostly. Weeks of watching sounded, on its face, like the safest assignment this mission had handed him yet, and some old, tired part of him was grateful enough for that safety not to examine the rest of what he was feeling too closely tonight.

He rose from the chair carefully, testing his own balance before he trusted it fully, and found it holding better now than it had on the climb up. Iofiel had already returned her attention to the star-map, and Raphael stood a little apart from both of them, still watching the sealed fragment on the table's surface with an expression Sariel couldn't quite name. He wasn't certain he'd rest easily himself, whatever Gabriel intended to call the coming weeks.

He said nothing about the small, unfamiliar restlessness already settling into him at the word patience — the strange, quiet discovery that danger, for a man built the way he was built, had always been easier to carry than waiting had ever once promised to be. He kept that discovery to himself, and said nothing further to correct whatever impression his silence left Gabriel with.

Chapter 27

The Wandering Bard

Milo Ray sat cross-legged on the grass near the university's main gate, barefoot despite the cool of the afternoon, his cracked guitar balanced across one knee like something too familiar to bother holding carefully. His sun-bleached hair had gone loose from whatever knot he'd tied it into that morning, and he hadn't bothered fixing it, letting it fall where it wanted the same way he let most things about his own appearance fall where they wanted. He played without any particular audience in mind, the way he always played, letting a chord find its own shape before he decided whether to follow it or abandon it for another.

A withered flower drooped in a chipped clay pot someone had left forgotten on the low wall beside him, its petals brown and curled at the edges, abandoned there by some student who'd clearly given up on it weeks ago and never quite gotten around to throwing it out. Milo let his fingers land on a chord that had nothing especially remarkable about it to anyone listening.

The flower straightened. Color bled back into its petals in a slow, unhurried bloom, as though it had simply been waiting all this time for someone to remind it that blooming was still an option. Milo didn't look at it directly. He never did.
